Crystal bases of modified ı \imath quantum groups of certain quasi-split types
Hideya Watanabe

Abstract
. In order to see the behavior of ı \imath canonical bases at q = ∞ q=\infty , we introduce the notion of ı \imath crystals associated to an ı \imath quantum group of certain quasi-split type. The theory of ı \imath crystals clarifies why ı \imath canonical basis elements are not always preserved under natural homomorphisms. Also, we construct a projective system of ı \imath crystals whose projective limit can be thought of as the ı \imath canonical basis of the modified ı \imath quantum group at q = ∞ q=\infty .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 17B37; Secondary 17B10

1.
Introduction

The aim of this paper is to introduce the notion of
\imath
crystals associated to a quantum symmetric pair
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{U}^{\imath})
of certain quasi-split type. This study is motivated by the theory of canonical bases for quantum symmetric pairs (also known as the theory of
\imath
canonical bases) initiated by Bao and Wang in
[
1
]
and developed in
[
2
,
3
]
.

Let
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
be a Satake diagram of symmetrizable Kac-Moody type (also known as an admissible pair). Namely,
I
is a Dynkin diagram of symmetrizable Kac-Moody type,
I_{\bullet}\subset I
is a subdiagram of finite type, and
\tau\in\operatorname{Aut}(I)
is a diagram automorphism of order at most two, satisfying certain axioms. From our Dynkin diagram
I
, we can construct the quantum group
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}_{I}=U_{q}(I)
. It has a set of generators
E_{i},F_{i},K_{h}
,
i\in I
,
h\in Y
, where
Y
denotes the coroot lattice. In
[
10
]
, Kolb defined a right coideal subalgebra
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}=\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\subset\mathbf{U}
in terms of
I_{\bullet}
and
\tau
, where
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=(\varsigma_{i})_{i\in I}\in(\mathbb{C}(q)^{\times})^{I}
and
\boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathbb{C}(q)^{I}
are parameters. To be more a bit precise,
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is generated by the quantum group
\mathbf{U}_{I_{\bullet}}
associated to the subdiagram
I_{\bullet}
,
K_{h}
for
h\in Y^{\imath}:=\{h\in Y\mid w_{\bullet}\tau(h)=-h\}
, and distinguished elements
B_{i}
for
i\in I{\setminus}I_{\bullet}
. The pair
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{U}^{\imath})
is called the quantum symmetric pair associated to our Satake diagram
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
and parameters
\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\kappa}
. The coideal subalgebra
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
itself is referred to as the
\imath
quantum group. Kolb’s construction generalizes Letzter’s construction
[
12
]
for
I
being finite type, which unifies earlier examples constructed by Koornwinder
[
11
]
, Gavrilik-Klimyk
[
8
]
, Noumi
[
15
]
and others.

It has turned out that the theory of quantum symmetric pairs has many applications in numerous areas of mathematics and physics such as representation theory of Lie algebras, orthogonal polynomials, low-dimensional topology, categorifications, and integrable systems. Such applications are often based on the fact that the
\imath
quantum group
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
can be thought of as a generalization of the quantum group. To be more precise, when we take a Satake diagram of diagonal type, the embedding
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{U}
can be identified with the comultiplication map
\Delta:\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
for some Dynkin diagram
I^{\prime}
(see Example
3.1.1
for details).

From this point of view, the theory of
\imath
canonical bases are thought of as a generalization of the theory of canonical bases for quantum groups initiated by Lusztig
[
13
]
. Namely, the theory of
\imath
canonical bases for diagonal types recovers the usual theory of canonical bases. Let us see this in more detail. Let
X=X_{I}
denote the weight lattice, and set
X^{\imath}:=X/\{\lambda+w_{\bullet}\tau(\lambda)\mid\lambda\in X\}
, where
w_{\bullet}
denotes the longest element of the Weyl group associated to
I_{\bullet}
(recall that
I_{\bullet}
is of finite type). Then, the modified
\imath
quantum group is defined as

\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}:=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}.

Here,
\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
’s are orthogonal idempotents. When our Satake diagram is of diagonal type, we can identify
X
with
X_{I^{\prime}}\oplus X_{I^{\prime}}
and
X^{\imath}
with
X_{I^{\prime}}
, and hence, the modified
\imath
quantum group is identified with the modified quantum group
\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{I^{\prime}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X_{I^{\prime}}}\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
(recall that we have identified
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}=\Delta(\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}})\simeq\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
).

For each dominant integral weight
\lambda\in X^{+}
, let
V(\lambda)
(resp.,
V^{\rm{low}}(\lambda)
) denote the irreducible highest weight module of highest weight
\lambda
, and
v_{\lambda}\in V(\lambda)
the highest weight vector (resp., irreducible lowest weight module of lowest weight
-\lambda
, and
v^{\rm{low}}_{\lambda}
the lowest weight vector). Given
\lambda,\mu\in X^{+}
, let
V^{\imath}(\lambda,\mu)
denote the
\mathbf{U}
-submodule of
V(\lambda)\otimes V(\mu)
generated by
v_{w_{\bullet}(\lambda)}\otimes v_{\mu}
, where
v_{w_{\bullet}(\lambda)}\in V(\lambda)
denotes the canonical basis element of weight
w_{\bullet}(\lambda)
. Then, Bao and Wang
[
2
,
3
]
proved that
V^{\imath}(\lambda,\mu)
has a distinguished basis, which they called the
\imath
canonical basis, of the form

\mathbf{B}^{\imath}(\lambda,\mu)=\{(b_{1}\diamond b_{2})^{\imath}_{w_{\bullet}(\lambda),\mu}\mid b_{1}\in\mathbf{B}_{I_{\bullet}},\ b_{2}\in\mathbf{B}\}{\setminus}\{0\}.

Here,
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}_{I}
denotes the canonical basis of the Lusztig algebra
\mathbf{f}
associated to
I
. Also, they constructed a projective system of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules

V^{\imath}(\lambda+\tau(\nu),\mu+\nu)\rightarrow V^{\imath}(\lambda,\mu)

which sends
v_{w_{\bullet}(\lambda+\tau(\nu))}\otimes v_{\mu+\nu}
to
v_{w_{\bullet}(\lambda)}\otimes v_{\mu}
. Although it is not always true that this morphism is based (i.e., it sends an
\imath
canonical basis element to either an
\imath
canonical basis element or zero), Bao and Wang were able to prove that it is asymptotically true. Namely, given
b_{1}\in\mathbf{B}_{I_{\bullet}}
,
b_{2}\in\mathbf{B}
, if we take
\lambda,\mu\in X^{+}
to be sufficiently dominant, then the
\imath
canonical basis element
(b_{1}\diamond b_{2})^{\imath}_{w_{\bullet}(\lambda+\tau(\nu)),\mu+\nu}
is sent to
(b_{1}\diamond b_{2})^{\imath}_{w_{\bullet}(\lambda),\mu}
for all
\nu\in X^{+}
. Furthermore, they constructed the
\imath
canonical basis
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{\imath}
of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
as an asymptotical limit of this projective system (see Theorem
3.2.7
for the precise meaning).

When our Satake diagram is of diagonal type, the
\mathbf{U}
-module
V^{\imath}(\lambda,\mu)
is just the tensor product
V^{\rm{low}}(\lambda^{\prime})\otimes V(\mu^{\prime})
for some
\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\in X_{I^{\prime}}^{+}
, and its
\imath
canonical basis coincides with the canonical basis seen as a tensor product
\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
-module. In this case, the morphisms in the projective system are based.

Thus, we are led to investigate why and how much this property fails for quantum symmetric pairs beyond diagonal type. In this paper, we shall take a crystal theoretic approach. Namely, we focus on the projective system at
q=\infty
.

Until the end of this Introduction, assume that our Satake diagram is quasi-split (i.e.,
I_{\bullet}=\emptyset
), and the Cartan matrix
(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
satisfies
a_{i,\tau(i)}\in\{2,0,-1\}
for all
i\in I
. Note that every Satake diagram of diagonal type satisfies this assumption. Earlier works
[
16
,
18
,
19
]
of the author suggest that there is a good combinatorial theory which has much information of the representation theory of the
\imath
quantum group associated to our Satake diagram and special parameters
\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\kappa}
. In this paper, for each
i\in I
, we define a linear operator
\widetilde{B}_{i}
acting on certain
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules which modifies the action of
B_{i}
(recall that
B_{i}
is one of the generators of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
). Such an operator
\widetilde{B}_{i}
for
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=2
(resp.,
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
) has been introduced in
[
18
]
(resp.,
[
16
]
). The
\widetilde{B}_{i}
for
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1
is new, and it generalizes the operator introduced in
[
16
]
. As the Kashiwara operators in the representation theory of quantum groups, our operators
\widetilde{B}_{i}
are defined in a way such that we can take the crystal limit, i.e., the
q\rightarrow\infty
limit. When our Satake diagram is of diagonal type, the
\widetilde{B}_{i}
coincides with a Kashiwara operator for
\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
.

Then, we investigate how the operators
\widetilde{B}_{i}
act on the tensor product of a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module on which
\widetilde{B}_{i}
’s are defined, and a
\mathbf{U}
-module on which Kashiwara operators are defined (recall that
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is a right coideal of
\mathbf{U}
). Taking the crystal limit, we obtain a combinatorial tensor product rule for
\widetilde{B}_{i}
. As a special case, let us consider the tensor product of the trivial
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module and a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
. Such a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module is canonically identified with
M
regarded as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module by restriction. Then, the tensor product rule for
\widetilde{B}_{i}
gives a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module structure of
M
at
q=\infty
. When our Satake diagram is of diagonal type, the
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
is of the form
L\otimes N
for some
\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
-modules
L,N
. Then,
\widetilde{B}_{i}
on
M=L\otimes N
at
q=\infty
coincides with the tensor product rule for a Kashiwara operator for
\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
.

Following the theory of crystals, we introduce the notion of
\imath
crystals which abstract the operators
\widetilde{B}_{i}
at
q=\infty
. An
\imath
crystal is a set
\mathcal{B}
equipped with several structure maps including linear operators
\widetilde{B}_{i}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}\mathcal{B})
,
i\in I
. We prove that given an
\imath
crystal
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and a crystal
\mathcal{B}_{2}
satisfying certain conditions, the tensor product
\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}:=\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{2}
of them has an
\imath
crystal structure. Taking
\mathcal{B}_{1}
to be “the trivial
\imath
crystal”, we obtain a way making a crystal into an
\imath
crystal. As a result, it turns out that the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module structure of a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
at
q=\infty
is described by the crystal basis of
M
regarded as an
\imath
crystal via this method.

Let us return to the projective system
\{V^{\imath}(\lambda,\mu)\}_{\lambda,\mu\in X^{+}}
. Under the assumption on our Satake diagram, the
\mathbf{U}
-module
V^{\imath}(\lambda,\mu)
is isomorphic to the irreducible highest weight module
V(\lambda+\mu)
. Hence, the projective system becomes
\{V(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in X^{+}}
with morphisms
V(\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu))\rightarrow V(\nu)
sending
v_{\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu)}
to
v_{\nu}
. Now, we are able to answer our question why and how much the morphisms
V(\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu))\rightarrow V(\lambda)
are not based. That is, when
\lambda
is not sufficiently dominant, there is no morphism
\mathcal{B}(\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu))\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
of
\imath
crystals sending the highest weight element
b_{\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu)}
to the highest weight element
b_{\lambda}
, where
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
denotes the crystal basis of
V(\lambda)
. Via a further observation, it turns out that there exists a dominant weight
\sigma\in X^{+}
such that for each
\lambda,\nu\in X^{+}
, there exists a morphism
\mathcal{B}(\sigma+\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu);\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu))\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\sigma+\lambda;\lambda)
of
\imath
crystals sending
b_{\sigma+\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu)}
to
b_{\sigma+\lambda}
, where

\mathcal{B}(\lambda;\mu):=\{\widetilde{F}_{i_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{i_{r}}b_{\lambda}\mid\widetilde{F}_{i_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{i_{r}}b_{\mu}\in\mathcal{B}(\mu)\}{\setminus}\{0\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\lambda).

Also, for each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, there exists an
\imath
crystal
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)^{\sigma}
isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}(\sigma+\lambda;\lambda)
whose underlying set is
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
. Thus, we obtain a projective system
\{\mathcal{B}(\lambda)^{\sigma}\}_{\lambda\in X^{+},\overline{\sigma+\lambda}=\zeta}
of
\imath
crystals for each
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
, where
\overline{\lambda}\in X^{\imath}
denote the image of
\lambda\in X
. This projective system has the projective limit of the form

\mathcal{T}_{\zeta}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\infty),

where
\mathcal{T}_{\zeta}
is a certain
\imath
crystal consisting of a single element, and
\mathcal{B}(\infty)
denotes the crystal basis of the negative part
\mathbf{U}^{-}
of
\mathbf{U}
.

Then, we lift these results to the representation theory of
\imath
quantum groups. Namely, for each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, we construct a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(\lambda)^{\sigma}
whose underlying set is
V(\lambda)
. The
V(\lambda)^{\sigma}
has a distinguished basis, called the
\imath
canonical basis, whose crystal limit is the
\imath
crystal
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)^{\sigma}
. Furthermore, there exists a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism
V(\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu))^{\sigma}\rightarrow V(\lambda)^{\sigma}
which sends
v_{\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu)}
to
v_{\lambda}
. Thus, we obtain a projective system
\{V(\lambda)^{\sigma}\}_{\lambda\in X^{+},\overline{\sigma+\lambda}=\zeta}
of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules and based homomorphisms. In particular, when we can take
\sigma
to be
0
, this result partially proves Bao and Wang’s Conjecture in
[
2
, Remark 6.18]
. It turns out that this projective system has the projective limit, and it is
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
with the
\imath
canonical basis
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{\imath}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
.

From the results above, we should call the
\imath
crystal
\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathcal{T}_{\zeta}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\infty)
the
\imath
crystal basis of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
, and denote it by
\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{\imath}
. The description
\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{\imath}=\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathcal{T}_{\zeta}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\infty)
can be interpreted as the crystal limit of the description
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
since
\mathcal{T}_{\zeta}
consists of a single element, and
\mathcal{B}(\infty)
can be seen as the crystal limit of
\mathbf{U}^{-}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
as a vector space. When our Satake diagram is of diagonal type, we can take
\sigma=0
, and can identify
\mathcal{B}(\infty)=\mathcal{B}(-\infty)_{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\infty)_{I^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{B}(-\infty)_{I}
denotes the crystal basis of the positive part of
\mathbf{U}
. Hence, we recover the projective system
\{V^{\rm{low}}(\lambda)\otimes V(\mu)\}_{\lambda,\mu\in X_{I^{\prime}}^{+},-\lambda+\mu=\zeta}
of
\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
-modules and based homomorphisms, whose projective limit is
\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{I^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
, and the projective system
\{\mathcal{B}^{\rm{low}}(\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mu)\}_{\lambda,\mu\in X_{I^{\prime}}^{+},-\lambda+\mu=\zeta}
of crystals, whose projective limit is
\mathcal{B}(-\infty)_{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\zeta}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\infty)_{I^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{\zeta}
denotes a certain crystal consisting of a single element, and
\mathcal{B}^{\rm{low}}(\lambda)
the crystal basis of
V^{\rm{low}}(\lambda)
.

As explained above, our construction of the new projective system
\{V(\lambda)^{\sigma}\}_{\lambda\in X^{+}}
is motivated by an observation of the
\imath
crystal structures of various
\mathbf{U}
-modules. However, the construction itself may be possible without the theory of
\imath
crystals and our assumption on Satake diagrams and parameters
\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\kappa}
. We will treat this in a future work.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall necessary knowledge concerning ordinary quantum groups and crystals. In Section
3
, we set up an
\imath
quantum group of quasi-split type, and recall Bao-Wang’s construction of the projective system and the
\imath
canonical basis of the modified
\imath
quantum group. Also, we define the notion of
\imath
crystals and their morphisms. Basic examples of
\imath
crystals are given there. Section
4
is devoted to defining the operators
\widetilde{B}_{i}
acting on certain
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules. The tensor product rule is also investigated. Based on the results obtained there, we define the tensor product of an
\imath
crystal and a crystal in Section
5
. The associativity of this tensor product is stated there, too. The proofs of the well-definedness and the associativity of the tensor product is given in Section
7
because they are lengthy and independent of later argument. In Section
6
, we construct the projective system of
\imath
crystals and its projective limit mentioned earlier. Then, we finally construct our new projective system of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules and based homomorphisms.

Notation

Throughout this paper, we use the following notation:

•

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}(q)
: the field of rational functions in one variable
q
.

•

\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
: the subring of
\mathbb{K}
consisting of functions regular at
q=\infty
.

•

\mathbf{A}:=\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
.

•

For
a,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
[n]_{q^{a}}:=\frac{q^{an}-q^{-an}}{q^{a}-q^{-a}}
,
[n]_{q^{a}}!:=\prod_{k=1}^{n}[k]_{q^{a}}
,
{m+n\brack n}_{q^{a}}:=\frac{[m+n]_{q^{a}}!}{[m]_{q^{a}}![n]_{q^{a}}!}
.

•

\overline{n}\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
: the image of
n\in\mathbb{Z}
under the quotient map
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

•

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\operatorname{sgn}(n):=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }n>0,\\
0&\text{ if }n=0,\\
-1&\text{ if }n<0.\end{cases}

2.
Quantum groups and crystals

In this section, we review basic results concerning the representation theory of quantum groups associated to a symmetrizable Kac-Moody algebra, abstract crystals, and canonical and crystal bases.

2.1.
Quantum groups

Let
A=(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
be a symmetrizable generalized Cartan matrix with symmetrizing matrix
D=\operatorname{diag}(d_{i})_{i\in I}
, i.e.,
d_{i}
’s are pairwise coprime positive integers satisfying
d_{i}a_{i,j}=d_{j}a_{j,i}
for all
i,j\in I
. Let
X,Y
be free abelian groups of finite rank equipped with a perfect pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:Y\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subset X
and
\{h_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subset Y
be linearly independent sets such that

\langle h_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle=a_{i,j}\quad\text{ for all }i,j\in I.

When
I
is of finite type, each
\lambda\in X
is uniquely determined by the values
\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
,
i\in I
. We often identify
\lambda\in X
with
(\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle)_{i\in I}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}
.

The quantum group
\mathbf{U}
is defined to be a unital associative algebra over
\mathbb{K}
generated by
E_{i},F_{i},K_{h}
,
i\in I
,
h\in Y
subject to the following relations: Let
i,j\in I
,
h,h^{\prime}\in Y
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&K_{0}=1,\quad K_{h}K_{h^{\prime}}=K_{h+h^{\prime}},\\
&K_{h}E_{i}=q^{\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle}E_{i}K_{h},\quad K_{h}F_{i}=q^{-\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle}F_{i}K_{h},\\
&E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}},\\
&\sum_{r=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{r}E_{i}^{(r)}E_{j}E_{i}^{(1-a_{i,j}-r)}=0\quad\text{ if }i\neq j,\\
&\sum_{r=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{r}F_{i}^{(r)}F_{j}F_{i}^{(1-a_{i,j}-r)}=0\quad\text{ if }i\neq j,\end{split}

where

q_{i}:=q^{d_{i}},\ K_{i}:=K_{d_{i}h_{i}},\ E_{i}^{(a)}:=\frac{1}{[a]_{i}!}E_{i}^{a},\ F_{i}^{(a)}:=\frac{1}{[a]_{i}!}F_{i}^{a},\ [a]_{i}:=[a]_{q_{i}},\ [a]_{i}!:=[a]_{q_{i}}!.

The quantum group
\mathbf{U}
is equipped with a Hopf algebra structure with comultiplication
\Delta
given by

\Delta(E_{i}):=E_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}\otimes E_{i},\ \Delta(F_{i}):=1\otimes F_{i}+F_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{-1},\ \Delta(K_{h}):=K_{h}\otimes K_{h}.

There is an anti-algebra involution
\wp
on
\mathbf{U}
such that

\wp(E_{i})=q_{i}^{-1}F_{i}K_{i},\ \wp(F_{i})=q_{i}^{-1}E_{i}K_{i}^{-1},\ \wp(K_{h})=K_{h}.

The complex conjugate
\mathbb{C}\ni z\mapsto z^{*}
can be extended to an
\mathbb{R}(q)
-algebra automorphism on
\mathbf{U}
by requiring

E_{i}^{*}=E_{i},\ F_{i}^{*}=F_{i},\ K_{h}^{*}=K_{h}.

Set
\wp^{*}:=\wp\circ*=*\circ\wp
.

Let
\overline{\cdot}:\mathbb{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
denote the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra automorphism given by
\overline{q}=q^{-1}
. This can be extended to a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra automorphism
\psi
, called the bar-involution on
\mathbf{U}
by

\psi(E_{i})=E_{i},\ \psi(F_{i})=F_{i},\ \psi(K_{h})=K_{-h}.

For each
J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\}\subset I
of finite type, let
\mathbf{U}_{J}=\mathbf{U}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
E_{j},F_{j},K_{j}^{\pm 1}
,
j\in J
.

Let
\mathbf{U}^{-}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
F_{i}
,
i\in I
. Also, let
\mathbf{B}(\infty)
and
\mathcal{B}(\infty)
denote the canonical and crystal basis of
\mathbf{U}^{-}
with
b_{\infty}\in\mathcal{B}(\infty)
the highest weight element.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{U}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
denote the modified quantum group, and
\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{A}}
its
\mathbf{A}
-form. Also, let
\dot{\mathbf{B}}
and
\dot{\mathcal{B}}
denote the canonical and crystal basis of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
, respectively.

For each
\lambda\in X
, let
M(\lambda)
denote the Verma module of highest weight
\lambda
, and
V(\lambda)
its irreducible quotient. Let
v_{\lambda}
denote the highest weight vector of both
M(\lambda)
and
V(\lambda)
. When
\lambda\in X^{+}
, let
\mathbf{B}(\lambda)
and
(\mathcal{L}(\lambda),\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
denote the canonical and crystal base of
V(\lambda)
. Let
b_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
denote the highest weight element.

2.2.
Crystal

A crystal is a set
\mathcal{B}
equipped with the following structure

•

\operatorname{wt}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow X
: map,

•

\varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\sqcup\{-\infty\}
: maps,
i\in I
, where
-\infty
is a formal symbol,

•

\widetilde{E}_{i},\widetilde{F}_{i}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\sqcup\{0\}
: maps,
i\in I
, where
0
is a formal symbol,

satisfying the following axioms: Let
b\in\mathcal{B}
and
i\in I
.

(1)

If
\varphi(b)=-\infty
, then
\widetilde{E}_{i}b=0=\widetilde{F}_{i}b
.

(2)

\varphi_{i}(b)=\varepsilon_{i}(b)+\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b)
, where
\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b):=\langle h_{i},\operatorname{wt}(b)\rangle
; we understand that
-\infty+a=-\infty
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

If
\widetilde{E}_{i}b\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{wt}(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)=\operatorname{wt}(b)+\alpha_{i}
,
\varepsilon_{i}(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)=\varepsilon_{i}(b)-1
, and
\widetilde{F}_{i}\widetilde{E}_{i}b=b
.

(4)

If
\widetilde{F}_{i}b\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{wt}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\operatorname{wt}(b)-\alpha_{i}
,
\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{i}(b)-1
, and
\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{i}b=b
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a crystal. The crystal graph of
\mathcal{B}
is an
I
-colored directed graph whose vertex set is
\mathcal{B}
, and for each
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
and
i\in I
, there exists an arrow from
b
to
b^{\prime}
labeled by
i
if and only if
\widetilde{F}_{i}b=b^{\prime}
.

Example 2.2.1
.

Suppose that
I=\{i\}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
\mathcal{B}(n):=\{b_{k}\mid 0\leq k\leq n\}
denote the crystal given by

\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b_{k})=n-2k,\quad\varepsilon_{i}(b_{k})=k,\quad\varphi_{i}(b_{k})=n-k,\quad\widetilde{E}_{i}b_{k}=b_{k-1},\quad\widetilde{F}_{i}b_{k}=b_{k+1},

where
b_{-1}=b_{n+1}:=0
. The crystal graph of
\mathcal{B}(n)
is as follows:

\textstyle{b_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{n}.}

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}
be crystals. A morphism
\mu:\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{2}
of crystals is a map
\mu:\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{2}\sqcup\{0\}
satisfying the following: Let
b\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
i\in I
.

(1)

If
\mu(b)\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{wt}(\mu(b))=\operatorname{wt}(b)
,
\varepsilon_{i}(\mu(b))=\varepsilon_{i}(b)
, and
\varphi_{i}(\mu(b))=\varphi_{i}(b)
.

(2)

If
\widetilde{E}_{i}b,\mu(b),\mu(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)\neq 0
, then
\mu(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)=\widetilde{E}_{i}\mu(b)
.

(3)

If
\widetilde{F}_{i}b,\mu(b),\mu(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)\neq 0
, then
\mu(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\widetilde{F}_{i}\mu(b)
.

A crystal morphism
\mu:\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{2}
is said to be strict if
\mu(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)=\widetilde{E}_{i}\mu(b)
and
\mu(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\widetilde{F}_{i}\mu(b)
for all
i\in I
,
b\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
; here, we set
\mu(0)=0
. A strict crystal morphism
\mu:\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{2}
is said to be an isomorphism if the underlying map
\mu:\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{2}
is bijective; in this case, we denote
\mathcal{B}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}
be crystals. The tensor product
\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}
of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is a crystal whose underlying set is
\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{2}
and whose structure maps are given as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{wt}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\operatorname{wt}(b_{1})+\operatorname{wt}(b_{2}),\\
&\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\max(\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})-\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b_{2}),\varepsilon_{i}(b_{2}))=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})-\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b_{2})&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})>\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
\varepsilon_{i}(b_{2})&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})\leq\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\end{cases}\\
&\varphi_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\max(\varphi_{i}(b_{1}),\varphi_{i}(b_{2})+\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b_{1}))=\begin{cases}\varphi_{i}(b_{2})+\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b_{1})&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})<\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
\varphi_{i}(b_{1})&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})\geq\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{E}_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{E}_{i}b_{1}\otimes b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})>\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
b_{1}\otimes\widetilde{E}_{i}b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})\leq\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{F}_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\begin{cases}b_{1}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{i}b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})<\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
\widetilde{F}_{i}b_{1}\otimes b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})\geq\varphi_{i}(b_{2}).\end{cases}\end{split}

Here, we understand that
-\infty<a
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
-\infty\leq-\infty
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

The tensor product for crystals is associative
[
6
, Proposition 2.3.2]
.

Example 2.2.3
.

Suppose that
I=\{i\}
. Recall from Example
2.2.1
the crystal
\mathcal{B}(n)
. The crystal graph
\mathcal{B}(2)\otimes\mathcal{B}(3)
is described as follows:

\textstyle{b_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{3}}
\textstyle{b_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Let us introduce some terminologies. Let
\mathcal{B}
be a crystal.

(1)

\mathcal{B}
is said to be seminormal if

\varepsilon_{i}(b)=\max\{m\geq 0\mid\widetilde{E}_{i}^{m}b\neq 0\},\quad\varphi_{i}(b)=\max\{m\geq 0\mid\widetilde{F}_{i}^{m}b\neq 0\}

for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
and
i\in I
.

(2)

\mathcal{B}
is said to be upper seminormal if

\varepsilon_{i}(b)=\max\{m\geq 0\mid\widetilde{E}_{i}^{m}b\neq 0\}

for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
and
i\in I
.

Example 2.2.4
.

(1)

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, the crystal basis
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
of
V(\lambda)
is a seminormal crystal.

(2)

The crystal basis
\mathcal{B}(\infty)
of
\mathbf{U}^{-}
is an upper seminormal crystal. Also, we have
\widetilde{F}_{i}b\neq 0
for all
i\in I
and
b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty)
.

(3)

For each
\lambda\in X
, let
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}=\{t_{\lambda}\}
denote the crystal given by

\operatorname{wt}(t_{\lambda})=\lambda,\quad\varepsilon_{i}(t_{\lambda})=\varphi_{i}(t_{\lambda})=-\infty,\quad\widetilde{E}_{i}t_{\lambda}=\widetilde{F}_{i}t_{\lambda}=0,\quad\text{ for all }i\in I.

(4)

For each
\lambda,\mu\in X
, we have
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mu}\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\lambda+\mu}
.

(5)

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, there exists an injective crystal morphism
\iota_{\lambda}:\mathcal{B}(\lambda)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\infty)
such that
\iota_{\lambda}(b_{\lambda})=t_{\lambda}\otimes b_{\infty}
. This morphism is not strict.

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, set

\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda):=\{b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty)\mid t_{\lambda}\otimes b\in\operatorname{Im}\iota_{\lambda}\}.

Let
\pi_{\lambda}:\mathcal{B}(\infty)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\lambda)\sqcup\{0\}
be a map defined by

\pi_{\lambda}(b):=\begin{cases}b^{\prime}\quad&\text{ if }b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)\and\iota_{\lambda}(b^{\prime})=t_{\lambda}\otimes b,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }b\notin\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda).\end{cases}

Note that for each
b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
and
i\in I
, we have

(1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{wt}(b)=\operatorname{wt}(\pi_{\lambda}(b))-\lambda,\quad\varepsilon_{i}(b)=\varepsilon_{i}(\pi_{\lambda}(b)),\quad\varphi_{i}(b)=\varphi_{i}(\pi_{\lambda}(b))-\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle.

Lemma 2.2.5
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
,
b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
, and
i\in I
.

(1)

We have
\widetilde{E}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
if and only if
\varepsilon_{i}(b)>0
.

(2)

We have
\widetilde{F}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
if and only if
\varphi_{i}(b)>-\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle
.

Proof.

Set
b^{\prime}:=\pi_{\lambda}(b)\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
. Let us prove the first assertion. Since
\mathcal{B}(\infty)
is upper seminormal, the “only if” part is obvious. Hence, let us assume that
\varepsilon_{i}(b)>0
. Then, by identity (
1
),

\varepsilon_{i}(b^{\prime})=\varepsilon_{i}(b)>0.

Since
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
is seminormal, we obtain
\widetilde{E}_{i}b^{\prime}\neq 0
. Therefore, we have
\iota_{\lambda}(\widetilde{E}_{i}b^{\prime})\neq 0
, and hence,

\iota_{\lambda}(\widetilde{E}_{i}b^{\prime})=\widetilde{E}_{i}\iota_{\lambda}(b^{\prime})=\widetilde{E}_{i}(t_{\lambda}\otimes b)=t_{\lambda}\otimes\widetilde{E}_{i}b.

This implies that
\widetilde{E}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
, as desired.

Next, we prove the second assertion. Noting that
\widetilde{F}_{i}(t_{\lambda}\otimes b)=t_{\lambda}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{i}b\neq 0
, we see that we have
\widetilde{F}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
if and only if
\widetilde{F}_{i}b^{\prime}\neq 0
. Since
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
is seminormal, the latter condition is equivalent to that
\varphi_{i}(b^{\prime})>0
. By identity (
1
), this condition is, in turn, equivalent to that

\varphi_{i}(b)>-\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle.

Thus, the assertion follows.
∎

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{+}
be such that
\langle h_{i},\mu\rangle\leq\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle
for all
i\in I
. Then, we have
\mathcal{B}(\infty;\mu)\subset\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
.

Proof.

Let
b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\mu)
. Then, we can write as

b=\widetilde{F}_{i_{r}}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{i_{1}}b_{\infty}

for some
i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in I
. We show that
b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
by induction on
r\geq 0
. When
r=0
, we have
b=b_{\infty}\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
. Hence, assume that
r>0
. By induction hypothesis, we have
b^{\prime}:=\widetilde{E}_{i_{r}}b=\widetilde{F}_{i_{r-1}}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{i_{1}}b_{\infty}\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\lambda)
. By Lemma
2.2.5
(
2
), we obtain

\varphi_{i}(b^{\prime})>-\langle h_{i},\mu\rangle\geq-\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle.

This implies, again by Lemma
2.2.5
(
2
), that
\widetilde{F}_{i_{r}}b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(\infty,\lambda)
. This completes the proof.
∎

For each
\lambda,\mu\in X^{+}
such that
\langle h_{i},\mu\rangle\leq\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle
for all
i\in I
, set

\mathcal{B}(\lambda;\mu):=\pi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{B}(\infty;\mu)).

Note that by Lemma
2.2.6
,
\pi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{B}(\infty;\mu))
does not contain
0
. By identity (
1
), for each
b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda;\mu)
,
i\in I
, and
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(\infty;\mu)
with
\pi_{\lambda}(b^{\prime})=b
, we obtain

(2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{wt}(b)=\operatorname{wt}(\pi_{\mu}(b^{\prime}))+(\lambda-\mu),\quad\varepsilon_{i}(b)=\varepsilon_{i}(\pi_{\mu}(b^{\prime})),\quad\varphi_{i}(b)=\varphi_{i}(\pi_{\lambda}(b^{\prime}))+\langle h_{i},\lambda-\mu\rangle.

Lemma 2.2.7
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{+}
be such that
\langle h_{i},\mu\rangle\leq\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle
for all
i\in I
,
b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda;\mu)
, and
i\in I
.

(1)

We have
\widetilde{E}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda;\mu)
if and only if
\varepsilon_{i}(b)>0
.

(2)

We have
\widetilde{F}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda;\mu)
if and only if
\varphi_{i}(b)>\langle h_{i},\lambda-\mu\rangle
.

Proof.

The assertions follow from Lemma
2.2.5
and identity (
2
).
∎

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a crystal, and
i,j\in I
with
a_{i,j}=a_{j,i}\in\{0,-1\}
. Consider the following conditions (
cf.

[
6
, Chapter 4]
):

(S1)

If
b,\widetilde{E}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\varepsilon_{j}(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)-\varepsilon_{j}(b)\in\{0,-a_{i,j}\}
.

(S2)

If
b,\widetilde{E}_{j}\widetilde{E}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}
and
\varepsilon_{j}(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)=\varepsilon_{j}(b)
, then
\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{E}_{j}b=\widetilde{E}_{j}\widetilde{E}_{i}b
and
\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{E}_{j}b)=\varphi_{i}(b)
.

(S3)

If
b,\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{E}_{j}b,\widetilde{E}_{j}\widetilde{E}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}
,
\varepsilon_{j}(\widetilde{E}_{i}b)=\varepsilon_{j}(b)+1
, and
\varepsilon_{i}(\widetilde{E}_{j}b)=\varepsilon_{i}(b)+1
, then
\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{E}_{j}b\neq\widetilde{E}_{j}\widetilde{E}_{i}b
.

(S2)’

If
b,\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}
and
\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{j}(b)
, then
\widetilde{F}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{j}b=\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b
and
\varepsilon_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}b)=\varepsilon_{i}(b)
.

(S3)’

If
b,\widetilde{F}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{j}b,\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}
,
\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{j}(b)+1
, and
\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}b)=\varphi_{i}(b)+1
, then
\widetilde{F}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{j}b\neq\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b
.

Example 2.2.8
.

The crystals
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
,
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
, and
\mathcal{B}(\infty)
satisfy the conditions above.

Lemma 2.2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a crystal, and
i,j\in I
with
a_{i,j}=a_{j,i}\in\{0,-1\}
. Assume that
\mathcal{B}
satisfies conditions
(S1)
–
(S3)’
. Then, for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
, the following hold.

(1)

If
\widetilde{F}_{i}b\neq 0
, then
\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)-\varphi_{j}(b)\in\{0,-a_{i,j}\}
.

(2)

If
\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b\neq 0
and
\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{j}(b)+1
, then
\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{i}(b)-1
.

(3)

If
\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{E}_{j}b\neq 0
and
\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{E}_{j}b)=\varphi_{i}(b)
, then
\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{E}_{j}b)=\varphi_{j}(b)
.

(4)

If
\widetilde{F}_{i}b,\widetilde{E}_{j}b\neq 0
, then we have
\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{E}_{j}b)=\varphi_{i}(b)-1
if and only if
\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{j}(b)
.

Proof.

Let us prove the first assertion. We compute as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)-\varphi_{j}(b)&=(\varepsilon_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)+\langle h_{j},\operatorname{wt}(b)-\alpha_{i}\rangle)-(\varepsilon_{j}(b)+\langle h_{j},\operatorname{wt}(b)\rangle)\\
&=-(\varepsilon_{j}(\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)-\varepsilon_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b))-a_{i,j}.\end{split}

By condition (S1), applied to
\widetilde{F}_{i}b
, the last line of the identity above is either
0
or
-a_{i,j}
. This proves the assertion.

Let us prove the second assertion. Assume contrary that
\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)\neq\varphi_{i}(b)-1
. Since
\varphi_{i}(b)-1=\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)
, the first assertion implies that
a_{i,j}=-1
and

(3)

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)+1=\varphi_{i}(b).

Then, we have

\varepsilon_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)-\operatorname{wt}_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{i}(b)-(\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b)-1)=\varepsilon_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varepsilon_{i}(\widetilde{E}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b).

By condition (S2), this implies that

\widetilde{E}_{j}\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b=\widetilde{E}_{i}\widetilde{E}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b=b,

which, in turn, implies that

(4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b=\widetilde{F}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{j}b.

Then, using identity (
3
), we compute as

\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}b)=\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{j}b)+1=\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b)+1=\varphi_{i}(b)+1.

By condition (S3)’, this, together with our assumption that
\varphi_{j}(\widetilde{F}_{i}b)=\varphi_{j}(b)+1
, implies that

\widetilde{F}_{i}\widetilde{F}_{j}b\neq\widetilde{F}_{j}\widetilde{F}_{i}b,

which contradicts identity (
4
). Thus, the assertion follows.

The third assertion can be proved in a similar way to the second one. The fourth assertion follows from the second and third ones.
∎

2.3.
Based modules

A
\mathbf{U}
-module is said to have a bar-involution
\psi_{M}:M\rightarrow M
if

\psi_{M}(xv)=\psi(x)\psi_{M}(v)\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U},\ v\in M.

For example, each
V(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in X^{+}
has a unique bar-involution
\psi_{\lambda}
such that
\psi_{\lambda}(v_{\lambda})=v_{\lambda}
.

A weight module is a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
which possesses a weight space decomposition

M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}M_{\lambda},\quad M_{\lambda}:=\{v\in M\mid K_{h}v=q^{\langle h,\lambda\rangle}v\text{ for all }h\in Y\}.

Each weight module
M
admits a natural
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
-module structure. An
\mathbf{A}
-form of
M
is an
\mathbf{A}
-lattice
M_{\mathbf{A}}
such that
\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{A}}M_{\mathbf{A}}\subset M_{\mathbf{A}}
. For example,
V(\lambda)
is a weight module, and
V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}:=\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{A}}v_{\lambda}
is an
\mathbf{A}
-form.

An integrable module is a weight module
M
on which
E_{i},F_{i}
acts locally nilpotently for all
i\in I
.
For example,
V(\lambda)
is integrable. Let
M
be an integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module. Then, for each
J\subset I
of finite type, as a
\mathbf{U}_{J}
-module,
M
decomposes into the direct sum of finite-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{U}_{J}
-modules.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a
\mathbb{K}
-valued Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
, i.e., a
\mathbb{K}
-valued Hermitian form satisfying the following (cf.
[
18
, Definition 2.1.2]
):

•

For each
v\in M\setminus\{0\}
, there exist a positive real number
c
and an integer
d
such that
(v,v)\in cq^{2d}+q^{2d-1}\mathbb{C}[\![q^{-1}]\!]
.

•

For each
v\in M
, we have
(v,v)=0
if and only if
v=0
.

It is said to be contragredient if

(xu,v)_{M}=(u,\wp^{*}(x)v)_{M}\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U},\ u,v\in M.

For example,
V(\lambda)
possesses a unique contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda}
such that
(v_{\lambda},v_{\lambda})_{\lambda}=1
.

Given a
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-lattice
\mathcal{L}_{M}
of a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
M
, set

\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}:=\mathcal{L}_{M}/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{M},

and let
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}:\mathcal{L}_{M}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
denote the quotient map. For example, when
M
is a
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
, we can take

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{M}:=\{v\in M\mid(v,v)_{M}\in\mathbb{K}_{\infty}\}.

The inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
induces a
\mathbb{C}
-valued Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
. For example, the canonical basis
\mathbf{B}(\lambda)
of
V(\lambda)
forms an almost orthonormal basis of
V(\lambda)
, and hence, the free basis of
\mathcal{L}(\lambda):=\mathcal{L}_{V(\lambda)}
. Furthermore, the crystal basis
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\mathbf{B}(\lambda))
forms an orthonormal basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda):=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V(\lambda)}
.

Given an integrable module
M
, let
\widetilde{E}_{i},\widetilde{F}_{i}
,
i\in I
denote Kashiwara operators acting on it. If
M
possesses a contragredient Hermitian inner product, then Kashiwara operators preserve
\mathcal{L}_{M}
, and hence induce
\mathbb{C}
-linear operators on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
. Furthermore,
\widetilde{E}_{i}
and
\widetilde{F}_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
are adjoint to each other.

A crystal base of an integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
is a pair
(\mathcal{L}_{M},\mathcal{B}_{M})
consisting of a
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-lattice
\mathcal{L}_{M}
of
M
which is compatible with the weight space decomposition of
M
and is preserved by the Kashiwara operators, and a
\mathbb{C}
-basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
which is compatible with the weight space decomposition of
M
and which forms a seminormal crystal with respect to the Kashiwara operators.

A based
\mathbf{U}
-module is a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
with a bar-involution
\psi_{M}
, a crystal base
(\mathcal{L}_{M},\mathcal{B}_{M})
, and an
\mathbf{A}
-form
M_{\mathbf{A}}
satisfying the following:

(1)

The quotient map
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}:\mathcal{L}_{M}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}\cap\psi_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces; let
G
denote its inverse.

(2)

For each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, it holds that
\psi_{M}(G(b))=G(b)
.

Given a based
\mathbf{U}
-modules
M,N
with crystal bases
\mathcal{B}_{M},\mathcal{B}_{N}
, a
\mathbf{U}
-module homomorphism
f:M\rightarrow N
is said to be a based module homomorphism if
f(G(\mathcal{B}_{M}))\subset G(\mathcal{B}_{N})\sqcup\{0\}
and
\operatorname{Ker}f
is spanned by a subset of
G(\mathcal{B}_{M})
.

Example 2.3.1
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
. As we have seen above, the irreducible highest weight module
V(\lambda)
possesses a bar-involution
\psi_{\lambda}
, a crystal base
(\mathcal{L}(\lambda),\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
, and an
\mathbf{A}
-form
V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}
. With respect to these structures,
V(\lambda)
is a based module, and we have
\mathbf{B}(\lambda)=G(\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
.

3.
\imath
Quantum groups and
\imath
crystals of quasi-split types

In this section, we recall what the
\imath
quantum group of quasi-split type is, and formulate the notion of based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules in a similar way to based
\mathbf{U}
-modules. Also, we introduce the notion of
\imath
crystals, which is the fundamental tool in this paper.

3.1.
\imath
Quantum groups of quasi-split types

Let
\tau
be a Dynkin diagram involution on
I
, i.e.,
\tau
is a permutation on
I
such that
\tau^{2}=\operatorname{id}
and
a_{\tau(i),\tau(j)}=a_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in I
. We further assume that there exist automorphisms (also denoted by
\tau
) on
X,Y
such that
\tau(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{\tau(i)}
and
\tau(h_{i})=h_{\tau(i)}
for all
i\in I
, and
\langle\tau(h),\tau(\lambda)\rangle=\langle h,\lambda\rangle
for all
h\in Y
and
\lambda\in X
. For each
i\in I
, fix
\varsigma_{i}\in\mathbb{C}(q)^{\times}
and
\kappa_{i}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\kappa_{i}=0
unless
\tau(i)=i
and
a_{j,i}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for all
j\in I
with
\tau(j)=j
.

•

\varsigma_{i}=\varsigma_{\tau(i)}
if
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
.

Then, the associated
\imath
quantum group
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}=\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\kappa}}
is defined to be the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
B_{i},K_{h}
,
i\in I
,
h\in Y^{\imath}
, where

B_{i}:=F_{i}+\varsigma_{i}E_{\tau(i)}K_{i}^{-1}+\kappa_{i}K_{i}^{-1},\quad Y^{\imath}:=\{h\in Y\mid\tau(h)=-h\}.

Example 3.1.1
.

Suppose that our Satake diagram is of diagonal type, i.e.,
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
for all
i\in I
. Then, one can choose
I_{1},I_{2}\subset I
in a way such that
I=I_{1}\sqcup I_{2}
,
a_{i_{1},i_{2}}=0
for all
i_{1}\in I_{1}
,
i_{2}\in I_{2}
, and
\tau(I_{1})=I_{2}
. According to this decomposition, we obtain
X=X_{I_{1}}\oplus X_{I_{2}}
,
Y=Y_{I_{1}}\oplus Y_{I_{2}}
. For each
\lambda\in X
and
h\in Y
, we write
\lambda=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}
,
h=h_{1}+h_{2}
, where
\lambda_{i}\in X_{I_{i}}
,
h_{i}\in Y_{I_{i}}
. Let
I^{\prime}
be a copy of
I_{1}
, and let
I_{1}\rightarrow I^{\prime}:\ i\mapsto i^{\prime}
denote the isomorphism. This induces isomorphisms
X_{I_{1}}\rightarrow X_{I^{\prime}};\ \lambda\mapsto\lambda^{\prime}
and
Y_{I_{1}}\rightarrow Y_{I^{\prime}};\ h\mapsto h^{\prime}
. For each
i\in I^{\prime}
, set
i_{1}\in I_{1}
to be the preimage of
i
, and
i_{2}:=\tau(i_{1})\in I_{2}
. Then, there exists an isomorphism
\mathbf{U}\rightarrow\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
such that

E_{i}\mapsto\begin{cases}F_{i^{\prime}}\otimes 1&\text{ if }i\in I_{1},\\
1\otimes E_{\tau(i)^{\prime}}&\text{ if }i\in I_{2},\end{cases}\ F_{i}\mapsto\begin{cases}E_{i^{\prime}}\otimes 1&\text{ if }i\in I_{1},\\
1\otimes F_{\tau(i)^{\prime}}&\text{ if }i\in I_{2},\end{cases}\ K_{h}\mapsto K_{-h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes K_{\tau(h_{2})^{\prime}}.

For each
i\in I
, set
\varsigma_{i}=1
and
\kappa_{i}=0
. Then, the associated
\imath
quantum group is a subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}
generated by

B_{i_{1}}=E_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}\otimes E_{i},\quad B_{i_{2}}=1\otimes F_{i}+F_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{-1},\quad K_{h}\otimes K_{h}

for
i\in I^{\prime}
,
h\in Y_{I^{\prime}}
. Therefore, we have

\mathbf{U}^{\imath}=\Delta(\mathbf{U}_{I^{\prime}}).

For each
J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\}\subset I
of finite type such that
\tau(J)=J
, let
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{J}=\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
generated by
B_{j},K_{j}K_{\tau(j)}^{-1}
,
j\in J
.

A set of defining relations is known
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
: For
h,h^{\prime}\in Y^{\imath}
,
i\neq j\in I
,
\overline{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
,

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&K_{0}=1,\quad K_{h}K_{h^{\prime}}=K_{h+h^{\prime}},\\
&K_{h}B_{i}=q^{\langle h,-\alpha_{i}\rangle}B_{i}K_{h},\\
&\sum_{n=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{n}B_{i}^{(n)}B_{j}B_{i}^{(1-a_{i,j}-n)}=\delta_{\tau(i),j}\frac{(-1)^{a_{i,\tau(i)}}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}}B_{i}^{(-a_{i,\tau(i)})}\\
&\quad\cdot(q_{i}^{a_{i,\tau(i)}}(q_{i}^{-2};q_{i}^{-2})_{-a_{i,\tau(i)}}\varsigma_{\tau(i)}k_{i}-(q_{i}^{2};q_{i}^{2})_{-a_{i,\tau(i)}}\varsigma_{i}k_{i}^{-1})\quad\text{ if }\tau(i)\neq i,\\
&\sum_{n=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{n}B_{i,\overline{a_{i,j}+p}}^{(n)}B_{j}B_{i,\overline{p}}^{(1-a_{i,j}-n)}=0\quad\text{ if }\tau(i)=i,\end{split}

where

k_{i}:=K_{i}K_{\tau(i)}^{-1},\quad(x;x)_{n}:=\prod_{k=1}^{n}(1-x^{k}),\quad B_{i}^{(n)}:=\frac{1}{[n]_{i}!}B_{i}^{n},

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B_{i,\overline{0}}^{(n)}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{[2k+1]_{i}!}B_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(B_{i}^{2}-q_{i}\varsigma_{i}[2j]_{i}^{2})\quad&\text{ if }n=2k+1,\\
\frac{1}{[2k]_{i}!}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(B_{i}^{2}-q_{i}\varsigma_{i}[2j-2]_{i}^{2})\quad&\text{ if }n=2k,\end{cases}\\
&B_{i,\overline{1}}^{(n)}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{[2k+1]_{i}!}B_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(B_{i}^{2}-q_{i}\varsigma_{i}[2j-1]_{i}^{2})\quad&\text{ if }n=2k+1,\\
\frac{1}{[2k]_{i}!}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(B_{i}^{2}-q_{i}\varsigma_{i}[2j-1]_{i}^{2})\quad&\text{ if }n=2k.\end{cases}\end{split}

Let us introduce a family of
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules which will be one of the key ingredients in later argument.

Proposition 3.1.2
.

Let
\sigma\in X
be such that
\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\sigma\rangle=0
for all
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
. Then, there exists a
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(0)^{\sigma}=\mathbb{K}v_{0}^{\sigma}
such that

B_{i}v_{0}^{\sigma}=0,\quad K_{h}v_{0}^{\sigma}=q^{\langle h,\sigma\rangle}v_{0}^{\sigma}\quad\text{ for all }i\in I,h\in Y^{\imath}.

Proof.

The assertion follows from relations (
5
).
∎

Proposition 3.1.3
.

Let
\sigma\in X
with
\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\sigma\rangle=0
for all
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
, and
M
a
\mathbf{U}
-module. Then, for each
\lambda\in X
,
v\in M_{\lambda}
,
i\in I
, and
h\in Y^{\imath}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&K_{h}(v_{0}^{\sigma}\otimes v)=q^{\langle h,\sigma+\lambda\rangle}v_{0}^{\sigma}\otimes v,\\
&B_{i}(v_{0}^{\sigma}\otimes v)=v_{0}^{\sigma}\otimes(F_{i}+q_{i}^{-\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\sigma\rangle}\varsigma_{i}E_{\tau(i)}K_{i}^{-1})v.\end{split}

Proof.

By the definitions, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Delta(K_{h})=K_{h}\otimes K_{h},\\
&\Delta(B_{i})=B_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{-1}+1\otimes F_{i}+k_{i}^{-1}\otimes(\varsigma_{i}E_{\tau(i)}K_{i}^{-1}).\end{split}

Then, the assertion follows.
∎

This result shows that the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(0)^{\sigma}\otimes M
behaves much like
M
viewed as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}}
-module with weights shifted by
\sigma
, where
\varsigma^{\prime}_{i}=q_{i}^{-\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\sigma\rangle}\varsigma_{i}
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{i}=0
.

3.2.
Based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules

Let us further assume the following conditions on the parameters
\varsigma_{i},\kappa_{i}
:

(6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varsigma_{i},\kappa_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}],\\
&\overline{\kappa_{i}}=\kappa_{i},\\
&\varsigma_{\tau(i)}=q_{i}^{-a_{i,\tau(i)}}\overline{\varsigma_{i}}.\end{split}

This assumption ensures the existence of the
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}
on
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
and the
\imath
canonical basis
\mathbf{B}^{\imath}(\lambda)
of
V(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in X^{+}
(see
[
3
]
).

A
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
is said to have an
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}_{M}:M\rightarrow M
if

\psi^{\imath}_{M}(xv)=\psi^{\imath}(x)\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v)\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath},\ v\in M.

Example 3.2.1
.

(1)

The irreducible highest weight module
V(\lambda)
possesses a unique
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}_{\lambda}
such that
\psi^{\imath}_{\lambda}(v_{\lambda})=v_{\lambda}
.

(2)

The
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(0)^{\sigma}
in Proposition
3.1.2
possesses a unique
\imath
bar-involution
\psi_{0}^{\imath,\sigma}
such that
\psi_{0}^{\imath,\sigma}(v_{0}^{\sigma})=v_{0}^{\sigma}
.

Set
X^{\imath}:=X/\{\lambda+\tau(\lambda)\mid\lambda\in X\}
, and
\overline{\cdot}:X\rightarrow X^{\imath}
the quotient map. The perfect pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:Y\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
induces a bilinear pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:Y^{\imath}\times X^{\imath}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. For each
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
and
i\in I
with
\tau(i)=i
, the parity of
\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle
is independent of
\lambda\in X
satisfying
\overline{\lambda}=\zeta
. We call
\overline{\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle}\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
the value of
\zeta
at
i
.

Remark 3.2.2
.

When
I
is of finite type,
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
is uniquely determined by the values
\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\zeta\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
for
i\in I
with
\tau(i)\neq i
, and the values of
\zeta
at
i\in I
with
\tau(i)=i
. In this way, we often identify
\zeta
with an element of
(\mathbb{Z}\sqcup(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}))^{I}
.

A
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
is said to be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module if it has a decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta}
satisfying the following:

•

K_{h}v=q^{\langle h,\zeta\rangle}v
for all
h\in Y^{\imath}
,
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
,
v\in M_{\zeta}
.

•

B_{i}M_{\zeta}\subset M_{\zeta-\overline{\alpha_{i}}}
for all
i\in I
,
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
.

Such a decomposition is called an
X^{\imath}
-weight space decomposition.

Example 3.2.3
.

(1)

Let
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}M_{\lambda}
be a weight
\mathbf{U}
-module. Then, it is an
X^{\imath}
-weight module with
X^{\imath}
-weight space decomposition

M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta},\quad M_{\zeta}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in X\\
\overline{\lambda}=\zeta\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda}.

We call it the canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure of
M
.

(2)

The
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(0)^{\sigma}
possesses an
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure given by
V(0)^{\sigma}=(V(0)^{\sigma})_{\overline{\sigma}}
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
denote the modified
\imath
quantum group, and
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}
its
\mathbf{A}
-form. Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module. Then it has a natural
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module structure (
[
18
, Subsection 3.3]
). An
\mathbf{A}
-form of
M
is an
\mathbf{A}
-lattice
M_{\mathbf{A}}
such that
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}M_{\mathbf{A}}\subset M_{\mathbf{A}}
.

Example 3.2.4
.

(1)

V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}
is an
\mathbf{A}
-form of
V(\lambda)
as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module with the canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure.

(2)

If the
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(0)^{\sigma}
has an
\mathbf{A}
-form, it must be the subspace
V(0)^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}:=\mathbf{A}v_{0}^{\sigma}
. This is the case when, for example,
\varsigma_{i}=q_{i}^{-1}
and
\kappa_{i}=[s_{i}]_{i}
for some
s_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=2
(in this case, an explicit generating set of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}
is known
[
4
,
5
]
).

A based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module is an
X^{\imath}
-weight module
M
with an
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
, a
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-lattice
\mathcal{L}_{M}
, a
\mathbb{C}
-basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
, and an
\mathbf{A}
-form
M_{\mathbf{A}}
satisfying the following:

(1)

The quotient map
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}:\mathcal{L}_{M}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}\cap\psi^{\imath}_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces; let
G^{\imath}
denote its inverse.

(2)

For each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, it holds that
\psi^{\imath}_{M}(G^{\imath}(b))=G^{\imath}(b)
.

The Homomorphisms of based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules are defined in the same way as those of based
\mathbf{U}
-modules.

Example 3.2.5
.

(1)

As we have seen so far,
V(\lambda)
possesses an
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}_{\lambda}
, a
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-lattice
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
, a
\mathbb{C}
-basis
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
, and an
\mathbf{A}
-form
V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}
. With respect to theses structures,
V(\lambda)
is a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module, and we have
\mathbf{B}^{\imath}(\lambda)=G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
.

(2)

If the
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(0)^{\sigma}
has an
\mathbf{A}
-form, then it is a based module with respect to the
\imath
bar-involution
\psi_{0}^{\imath,\sigma}
, the
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-lattice
\mathcal{L}(0)^{\sigma}:=\mathbb{K}_{\infty}v_{0}^{\sigma}
, the
\mathbb{C}
-basis
\mathcal{B}(0)^{\sigma}:=\{b_{0}^{\sigma}:=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v_{0}^{\sigma})\}
, and the
\mathbf{A}
-form
V(0)^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}
. Then,
G^{\imath}(b_{0}^{\sigma})=v_{0}^{\sigma}
.

Proposition 3.2.6
.

Let
\sigma\in X
be such that
\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\sigma\rangle=0
for all
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
. Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
. Suppose that
V(0)^{\sigma}
has an
\mathbf{A}
-form. Then,
V(\lambda)^{\sigma}:=V(0)^{\sigma}\otimes V(\lambda)
is a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module with respect to an
\imath
bar-involution fixing
v_{\lambda}^{\sigma}
, a
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-lattice
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)^{\sigma}:=\mathcal{L}(0)^{\sigma}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
, and an
\mathbf{A}
-form
V(\lambda)^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}:=V(0)^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from
[
3
, Theorem 6.15]
.
∎

Let
\lambda,\nu\in X^{+}
. By
[
3
, Proposition 7.1]
, there exists
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

\pi=\pi_{\lambda,\nu}:V(\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu))\rightarrow V(\lambda)

such that

\pi(v_{\lambda+\nu+\tau(\nu)})=v_{\lambda}.

For each
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
, these homomorphisms form a projective system
\{V(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in X^{+},\overline{\lambda}=\zeta}
which is asymptotically stable in the following sense:

Theorem 3.2.7

(
[
3
, Theorem 7.2]
)
.

Let
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
and
b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty)
. Then, there exists a unique
G^{\imath}_{\zeta}(b)\in\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
such that

G^{\imath}_{\zeta}(b)v_{\lambda}=G^{\imath}(\pi_{\lambda}(b))

for all
\lambda\gg 0
with
\overline{\lambda}=\zeta
. Here,
\lambda\gg 0
means
\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle
is sufficiently large for all
i\in I
. Moreover,
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{\imath}:=\{G^{\imath}_{\zeta}(b)\mid\zeta\in X^{\imath},\ b\in\mathcal{B}(\infty)\}
forms a basis of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
.

The basis
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{\imath}
is called the
\imath
canonical basis of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
.

Although each
V(\lambda)
is a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module, the homomorphisms in the projective system above are not necessarily based.

3.3.
\imath
Crystal

From now on, we assume the following:

•

a_{i,\tau(i)}\in\{2,0,-1\}
for all
i\in I
.

•

\varsigma_{i}\in\{q_{i}^{a}\mid a\in\mathbb{Z}\}
for all
i\in I
.

•

\kappa_{i}\in\{[a]_{i}\mid a\in\mathbb{Z}\}
for all
i\in I
.

Note that the first condition is satisfied for all
I
of finite or affine type, except of type
A_{1}^{(1)}
with nontrivial
\tau
. The second condition, together with axiom (
6
) in the beginning of Subsection
3.2
, forces
\varsigma_{i}
to satisfy the following:

•

\varsigma_{i}=q_{i}^{-1}
if
a_{i,\tau(i)}=2
.

•

\varsigma_{i}=1
if
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
.

•

\varsigma_{i}\varsigma_{\tau(i)}=q_{i}
if
a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1
.

Therefore, we have

B_{i}=\begin{cases}F_{i}+q_{i}^{-1}E_{i}K_{i}^{-1}+[s_{i}]_{i}\quad&\text{ if }a_{i,\tau(i)}=2,\\
F_{i}+E_{\tau(i)}K_{i}^{-1}\quad&\text{ if }a_{i,\tau(i)}=0,\\
F_{i}+q_{i}^{s_{i}}E_{\tau(i)}K_{i}^{-1}\quad&\text{ if }a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1\end{cases}

for some
s_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
s_{i}+s_{\tau(i)}=1
for all
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1
.

Remark 3.3.1
.

Our assumption on the parameters
\varsigma_{i},\kappa_{i}
ensures that
\wp^{*}(\mathbf{U}^{\imath})=\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
by
[
2
, Proposition 4.6]
. In particular, we can talk about contragredient Hermitian inner product on
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules.

Definition 3.3.2
.

An
\imath
crystal is a set
\mathcal{B}
equipped with the following structure:

•

\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow X^{\imath}
: map.

•

\beta_{i}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\sqcup\{-\infty,-\infty_{\mathrm{ev}},-\infty_{\mathrm{odd}}\}
: map,
i\in I
, where
-\infty,-\infty_{\mathrm{ev}}
, and
-\infty_{\mathrm{odd}}
are formal symbols.

•

\widetilde{B}_{i}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\overline{\mathcal{L}})
,
i\in I
, where
\overline{\mathcal{L}}:=\mathbb{C}\mathcal{B}
.

•

(\cdot,\cdot)
: Hermitian inner product on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
making
\mathcal{B}
an orthonormal basis.

satisfying the following axioms: Let
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
,
i\in I
.

(1)

If
\beta_{i}(b)\notin\mathbb{Z}
, then
\widetilde{B}_{i}b=0
.

(2)

If
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b^{\prime})=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b)-\overline{\alpha_{i}}
.

(3)

If
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})=(b,\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b^{\prime})
.

(4)

If
\widetilde{B}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}\widetilde{B}_{i}b=b
.

(5)

If
a_{i,\tau(i)}=2
, then

(a)

\beta_{i}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}\sqcup\{-\infty_{\mathrm{ev}},-\infty_{\mathrm{odd}}\}
.

(b)

\overline{\beta_{i}(b)+s_{i}}=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)
, where
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)
denotes the value of
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b)
at
i
(see before Remark
3.2.2
). We understand that

-\infty_{\mathrm{ev}}+a=\begin{cases}-\infty_{\mathrm{ev}}&\text{ if }\overline{a}=\overline{0},\\
-\infty_{\mathrm{odd}}&\text{ if }\overline{a}=\overline{1},\end{cases}\ -\infty_{\mathrm{odd}}+a=\begin{cases}-\infty_{\mathrm{odd}}&\text{ if }\overline{a}=\overline{0},\\
-\infty_{\mathrm{ev}}&\text{ if }\overline{a}=\overline{1}\end{cases}

for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\overline{-\infty_{\mathrm{ev}}}=\overline{0}
,
\overline{-\infty_{\mathrm{odd}}}=\overline{1}
.

(c)

If
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)
.

(6)

If
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
, then

(a)

\beta_{i}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}\sqcup\{-\infty\}
.

(b)

\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)
, where
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b):=\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b)\rangle
.

(c)

If
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
b^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{i}b
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)-1
.

(7)

If
a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1
, then

(a)

\beta_{i}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}\sqcup\{-\infty\}
.

(b)

\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
or
\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}+1
, where
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b):=\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b)\rangle
.

(c)

If
\beta_{i}(b)\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
and
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
b^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{i}b
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime})-s_{i}
.

(d)

If
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})\neq 0
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime})-s_{i}
, then
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)-1
.

Let us fix a complete set
I_{\tau}
of representatives for the
\tau
-orbits on
I
.

Definition 3.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an
\imath
crystal. The crystal graph of
\mathcal{B}
is an
(I_{\tau}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})
-colored directed graph whose vertex set is
\mathcal{B}
, and for each
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
,
i\in I_{\tau}
, and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, there exists an arrow from
b
to
b^{\prime}
labeled by
(i,z)
if and only if
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})=z
. We often omit the label
z
when
z=1
.

Example 3.3.4
.

Suppose that our Satake diagram is of diagonal type. We retain the notation in Example
3.1.1
. If we set
I_{\tau}:=I_{2}
, then an
\imath
crystal and its crystal graph are nothing but a crystal and its crystal graph associated to the Dynkin diagram
I^{\prime}
. Under this identification,
\widetilde{B}_{i_{1}},\widetilde{B}_{i_{2}},\beta_{i_{1}},\beta_{i_{2}}
correspond to
\widetilde{E}_{i},\widetilde{F}_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}
, respectively for each
i\in I^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.3.5
.

Since
\mathcal{B}
is an orthonormal basis, we have

\widetilde{B}_{i}b=\sum_{b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})b^{\prime}

for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
. Hence, the maps
\widetilde{B}_{i}
,
i\in I_{\tau}
can be recovered from the crystal graph of
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 3.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an
\imath
crystal,
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
, and
i\in I
. Then, we have

(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})=(b,\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b^{\prime}).

Proof.

By Definition
3.3.2
(
3
), we have
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})=(b,\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b^{\prime})
if
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})\neq 0
. Now, suppose that
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime})=0
. Assume contrary that
(b,\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b^{\prime})\neq 0
. Then, by Definition
3.3.2
(
3
) again, we obtain

(b^{\prime},\widetilde{B}_{i}b)=(\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b^{\prime},b)\neq 0,

which is a contradiction. Thus, the proof completes.
∎

Remark 3.3.7
.

By Lemma
3.3.6
(see also Remark
3.3.5
), the maps
\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}
,
i\in I_{\tau}
can be recovered from the crystal graph.

Definition 3.3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}
be
\imath
crystals. A morphism
\mu:\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{2}
of
\imath
crystals is a linear map
\mu:\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
, where
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{j}}:=\mathbb{C}\mathcal{B}_{j}
, satisfying the following: Let
b_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
,
b_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
, and
i\in I
.

(1)

If
(\mu(b_{1}),b_{2})\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b_{2})=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b_{1})
and
\beta_{i}(b_{2})=\beta_{i}(b_{1})
.

(2)

If
\widetilde{B}_{i}b_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mu(b_{1}),\mu(\widetilde{B}_{i}b_{1})\neq 0
, then
\mu(\widetilde{B}_{i}b_{1})=\widetilde{B}_{i}\mu(b_{1})
.

An
\imath
crystal morphism
\mu:\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{2}
is said to be strict if
\mu(\widetilde{B}_{i}b)=\widetilde{B}_{i}\mu(b)
for all
i\in I
,
b\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
. A strict
\imath
crystal morphism is said to be an equivalence if the underlying map
\mu:\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
is a linear isomorphism; in this case, we write
\mathcal{B}_{1}\sim\mathcal{B}_{2}
. A strict
\imath
crystal morphism is said to be very strict if
\mu(\mathcal{B}_{1})\subset\mathcal{B}_{2}\sqcup\{0\}
. An
\imath
crystal equivalence is said to be an isomorphism if it is very strict; in this case, we write
\mathcal{B}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

Remark 3.3.9
.

Although an
\imath
crystal isomorphism induces an isomorphism of crystal graphs, it can happen that two equivalent
\imath
crystals have non-isomorphic crystal graphs.

Example 3.3.10
.

(1)

Consider the crystal basis
\mathcal{B}(0)=\{b_{0}\}
of the trivial module
V(0)
. It has an
\imath
crystal structure given as follows:

(a)

\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b_{0})=\overline{0}
.

(b)

\beta_{i}(b_{0})=\begin{cases}|s_{i}|\quad&\text{ if }a_{i,\tau(i)}=2,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }a_{i,\tau(i)}=0,\\
\max(-s_{i},0)\quad&\text{ if }a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1.\end{cases}

where
\zeta_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
denotes the value of

.

\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i,\operatorname{sgn}(n))}

.

Proposition 3.3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an
\imath
crystal,
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
, and
b\in\mathcal{B}
with
\widetilde{B}_{i}b\neq 0
. Then, we have
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(\widetilde{B}_{i}b)=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-2
and
\beta_{\tau(i)}(\widetilde{B}_{i}b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+1
.

Proof.

Set
b^{\prime}:=\widetilde{B}_{i}b\in\mathcal{B}
. Then, by Definition
3.3.2
(
2
), we have

\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime})=\langle h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)},\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}(b)-\overline{\alpha_{i}}\rangle=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-2.

This proves the first assertion. Next, by Definition
3.3.2
(
6c
), we have
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)-1
, and hence,

\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})-\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)-\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)+1=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+1.

This proves the second assertion.
∎

Proposition 3.3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an
\imath
crystal,
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1
, and
b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
with
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b,b^{\prime}),(\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b,b^{\prime\prime})\neq 0
. Then, the following hold:

(1)

\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime})=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-3
.

(2)

If
\beta_{i}(b)\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
, then
b^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{i}b
,
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)-1
, and
\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+2
.

(3)

If
\beta_{i}(b)\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime\prime})+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})-s_{i}
, then
b^{\prime\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b
,
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)+1
, and
\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)-2
.

(4)

If
\beta_{i}(b)\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime\prime})+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})-s_{i}
, then
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)+1
and
\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)-1
.

(5)

\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)+3
.

(6)

If
\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
, then
b^{\prime\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b
,
\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)-1
, and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)+2
.

(7)

If
\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime})-s_{i}
, then
b^{\prime}=\widetilde{B}_{i}b
,
\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+1
, and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)-2
.

(8)

If
\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})\neq\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})+\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime})-s_{i}
, then
\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)+1
and
\beta_{i}(b^{\prime})=\beta_{i}(b)-1
.

Proof.

The first assertion follows from Definition
3.3.2
(
2
) as Proposition
3.3.11
.

The second assertion follows from Definition
3.3.2
(
7b
) – (
7d
) and the first assertion of the proposition.

Let us prove the third assertion. By Lemma
3.3.6
, we have
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b^{\prime\prime},b)=(b^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b)\neq 0
. Then, the second assertion of the proposition implies that
b=\widetilde{B}_{i}b^{\prime\prime}
,
\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})-1
, and
\beta_{\tau(i)}(b)=\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime\prime})+2
. Now, by Definition
3.3.2
(
4
), we obtain

\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}b=\widetilde{B}_{\tau(i)}\widetilde{B}_{i}b^{\prime\prime}=b^{\prime\prime}.

Thus, the third assertion follows.

Let us prove the fourth assertion. Again, we have
(\widetilde{B}_{i}b^{\prime\prime},b)\neq 0
. Then, the first assertion of the proposition and Definition
3.3.2
(
7d
) imply that
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)=\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})-3
and
\beta_{i}(b)=\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})-1
, respectively. Now, we compute as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{\tau(i)}(b^{\prime\prime})&=\beta_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})-\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b^{\prime\prime})-s_{i}\\
&=(\beta_{i}(b)+1)-(\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)+3)-s_{i}\\
&=(\beta_{i}(b)-\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}_{i}(b)-s_{i}-1)-1\\
&=\beta_{i}(b)-1.\end{split}

Thus, the fourth assertion follows.

The remaining assertions follow from the first four assertions by interchanging the roles of
i
and
\tau(i)
.
∎

4.
Modified action of
B_{i}

For each
i\in I
, let
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{i}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
generated by
B_{i},B_{\tau(i)},(K_{i}K_{\tau(i)}^{-1})^{\pm 1}
.

In this section, we shall define linear operators
\widetilde{B}_{i}
,
i\in I
acting on
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules
M
satisfying the following.

(C1)

For each
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=2
, as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{i}
-module, the
M
is isomorphic to a direct sum of various
V^{\imath}(n)
to be defined in Subsection
4.1
.

(C2)

For each
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=0
, as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{i}
-module, the
M
is isomorphic to a direct sum of various
V^{\imath}(n)
to be defined in Subsection
4.2
.

(C3)

For each
i\in I
with
a_{i,\tau(i)}=-1
, as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{i}
-module, the
M
is isomorphic to a direct sum of various
V^{\imath}(n_{-},n_{+})
to be defined in Subsection
4.3
.

Also, for each
i\in I
, we give an decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}M_{i,k}
as a vector space, and define
\beta_{i}(m)
to be
k
for all
m\in M_{i,k}
.

Definition 4.0.1
.

Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module satisfying conditions (C1)–(C3) above. We say that a pair
(\mathcal{L}_{M},\mathcal{B}_{M})
is an
\imath
crystal base of
M
if
\mathcal{L}_{M}
is a
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-lattice of
M
and
\mathcal{B}_{M}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
satisfying the following:

•

\mathcal{L}_{M}=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathcal{L}_{M,\zeta}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{M,\zeta}:=\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\zeta}
.

•

\mathcal{B}_{M}=\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathcal{B}_{M,\zeta}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{M,\zeta}:=\mathcal{B}_{M}\cap\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\mathcal{L}_{M,\zeta})
; this enables us to define a map
\operatorname{wt}^{\imath}:\mathcal{B}_{M}\rightarrow X^{\imath}
.

•

\mathcal{L}_{M}
is stable under
\widetilde{B}_{i}
for all
i\in I
; this induces a linear map
\widetilde{B}_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
.

•

\mathcal{L}_{M}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{i,k})
, and
\mathcal{B}_{M}=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathcal{B}_{M}\cap\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{i,k}))
for all
i\in I
, where
M_{i,k}
denotes the subspace of
M
appearing in the decomposition above. This enables us to define a map
\beta_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.

•

\mathcal{B}_{M}
forms an
\imath
crystal with respect to the structure maps above and the Hermitian inner product making
\mathcal{B}_{M}
an orthonormal basis.

Also, we say that the
\mathcal{B}_{M}
is an
\imath
crystal basis of
M
.

Since the operators
\widetilde{B}_{i}
and maps
\beta_{i}
are defined in terms of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{i}
-modules, we assume, until the end of this section, that
I=\{i,\tau(i)\}
for some
i\in I_{\tau}
.

4.1.
The
a_{i,\tau(i)}=2
case

Suppose that
a_{i,\tau(i)}=2
. In this case, we have
\mathbf{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, and
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}=\mathbb{K}[B_{i}]
. Hence, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists a
1
-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V^{\imath}(n)=\mathbb{K}v
such that

B_{i}v=[n]_{i}v.

This
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module has an
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure such that
V^{\imath}(n)=V^{\imath}(n)_{\overline{n}}
; note that
X^{\imath}=X/2X\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

Set

\mathcal{L}^{\imath}(n):=\mathbb{K}_{\infty}v,\quad b:=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v),

and

\mathcal{B}^{\imath}(n):=\{b\}.

Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module isomorphic to a direct sum of
V^{\imath}(n)
’s. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
M[n]
denote the isotypic component of
M
of type
V^{\imath}(n)
. Then, we have

M=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}M[n].

For each
v\in M[n]
, we set

\beta_{i}(v):=|n|,\quad\widetilde{B}_{i}v:=\operatorname{sgn}(n)v.

Then, one can equip an
\imath
crystal structure on
\mathcal{B}^{\imath}(n)
.
Note that this
\imath
crystal is the same as the one in Example
3.3.10
(
3
).

Lemma 4.1.1
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the pair
(\mathcal{L}^{\imath}(n),\mathcal{B}^{\imath}(n))
is an
\imath
crystal base of
V^{\imath}(n)
. In particular, the crystal base
(\mathcal{L}(0),\mathcal{B}(0))
of the trivial
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(0)
is an
\imath
crystal base such that the
\imath
crystal structure of
\mathcal{B}(0)
is isomorphic to the
\imath
crystal
\mathcal{B}^{\imath}(s_{i})
.

Proof.

The first assertion is straightforwardly verified. The second assertion follows from the fact that
V(0)\simeq V^{\imath}(s_{i})
as
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules.
∎

Proposition 4.1.2
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module isomorphic to a direct sum of various
V^{\imath}(n)
with an
\imath
crystal base
(\mathcal{L}_{M},\mathcal{B}_{M})
, and
N
an integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module with a crystal base
(\mathcal{L}_{N},\mathcal{B}_{N})
. Then, the tensor product
M\otimes N
is isomorphic to a direct sum of various
V^{\imath}(n)
. Moreover, for each
b_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
,
b_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{N}
, the value
\beta_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})
and the vector
\widetilde{B}_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})
are well-defined; we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\beta_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\begin{cases}\beta_{i}(b_{1})-\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\beta_{i}(b_{1})>\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
\varepsilon_{i}(b_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\beta_{i}(b_{1})\leq\varphi_{i}(b_{2})\and\overline{\beta_{i}(b_{1})}=\overline{\varphi_{i}(b_{2})},\\
\varepsilon_{i}(b_{2})+1\quad&\text{ if }\beta_{i}(b_{1})\leq\varphi_{i}(b_{2})\and\overline{\beta_{i}(b_{1})}\neq\overline{\varphi_{i}(b_{2})},\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{B}_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{i}b_{1}\otimes b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\beta_{i}(b_{1})>\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
b_{1}\otimes\widetilde{E}_{i}b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\beta_{i}(b_{1})\leq\varphi_{i}(b_{2})\and\overline{\beta_{i}(b_{1})}=\overline{\varphi_{i}(b_{2})},\\
b_{1}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{i}b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\beta_{i}(b_{1})\leq\varphi_{i}(b_{2})\and\overline{\beta_{i}(b_{1})}\neq\overline{\varphi_{i}(b_{2})}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Proof.

The assertion can be proved essentially in the same way as
[
18
, Proposition 5.1.4]
.
∎

Corollary 4.1.3
.

Let
M
be an integrable
\mathbf{U}
-module with a crystal base
(\mathcal{L}_{N},\mathcal{B}_{N})
. Then, for each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, the value
\beta_{i}(b)
and the vector
\widetilde{B}_{i}(b)
are well-defined; we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\beta_{i}(b)=\begin{cases}|s_{i}|-\operatorname{wt}_{i}(b)\quad&\text{ if }|s_{i}|>\varphi_{i}(b),\\
\varepsilon_{i}(b)\quad&\text{ if }|s_{i}|\leq\varphi_{i}(b)\and\overline{s_{i}}=\overline{\varphi_{i}(b)},\\
\varepsilon_{i}(b)+1\quad&\text{ if }|s_{i}|\leq\varphi_{i}(b)\and\overline{s_{i}}\neq\overline{\varphi_{i}(b)},\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{B}_{i}(b)=\begin{cases}\operatorname{sgn}(s_{i})b\quad&\text{ if }|s_{i}|>\varphi_{i}(b),\\
\widetilde{E}_{i}(b)\quad&\text{ if }|s_{i}|\leq\varphi_{i}(b)\and\overline{s_{i}}=\overline{\varphi_{i}(b)},\\
\widetilde{F}_{i}(b)\quad&\text{ if }|s_{i}|\leq\varphi_{i}(b)\and\overline{s_{i}}\neq\overline{\varphi_{i}(b)}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Proof.

The assertion follows from Lemma
4.1.1
and Proposition
4.1.2
by identifying
\mathcal{B}_{M}
with
\mathcal{B}^{\imath}(s_{i})\otimes\mathcal{B}_{M}
.
∎

Example 4.1.4
.

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then, the
(n+1)

\mathbf{U}

V(n)
has
\mathcal{B}(n)
in Example
2.2.1

\widetilde{B}_{i}

” is omitted since we have
I_{\tau}=\{i\}
):

n<|s_{i}|
.

\textstyle{b_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

When

and
